### ["Farm strength" vs "breath awareness"](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/h99Wi5vfFbPasCFh5/farm-strength-vs-breath-awareness)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>If you want to become physically strong, the default solution to this problem is to go lift weights. The idea is that you can challenge your muscles in the gym, build capacity to develop force, and then next time you need to use strength </span><i><span>for real</span></i><span>, it'll be easier. And this works, obviously. Professional athletes lift weights for good reason, and it pays off when they have more strength with which to push back the opposing lineman or whatever.</span></p><p><span>However, this isn't the </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> way to build strength, and done poorly it can have serious downsides.</span></p><p><span>The alternative is to just </span><i><span>go do things that are hard. </span></i><span>Not because they're hard, but because they're worth doing </span><i><span>even though</span></i><span> they're hard. A farmer doesn't need to lift iron so that lifting bales of hay is easier, he can just lift the hay -- and if it's hard, that will build the strength that makes it easier. If nothing else, this saves him a gym membership and time by doing his strength training on the job.</span></p><p><span>There's another more interesting advantage though, which is that the feedback loop is tighter. If you're trying to lasso a bull and your grip strength is what's failing, guess which muscles are going to get the impetus to grow? If you're trying to lift a big rock into the back of a truck, not only does each muscle get its own growth signal in proportion to its need, you get practice </span><i><span>learning to use them</span></i><span>. There is skill in coordinating muscle contractions into the right postures to use muscles to do real things. There's skill in knowing when to </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> use muscles. While I'm sure NFL coaches do a pretty good job of matching training to the field use, it's easy to overemphasize bicep curls because biceps are what you notice on people that "look strong", and </span><a href="https://www.google.com/search?q=synthol+arms&amp;tbm=isch"><span>now you've invoked Goodhart's law</span></a><span>. I've had a woman I know tell me about guys at Jiu Jitsu who at first intimidated her with all their muscles, only for her to realize after rolling with them "Lol, those are </span><i><span>gym</span></i><span> muscles" — because these guys lacked the body awareness needed to actually use them effectively.</span></p><p><span>Figuring out what we're lifting for matters, because it determines what we lift and how we lift it. Even if we're just trying to impress people, it's hard to be more unimpressive than spending all our time building muscles that don't actually work when we need them.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>It is said that meditation is strength training for the mind. Why are we strength training our minds again? What is it that this strength is </span><i><span>for</span></i><span>, and are we keeping this in mind when we choose what and how we lift?</span></p><p><span>Meditation has all the same considerations and more, because staying connected to what you actually need meditation for is </span><i><span>itself</span></i><span> a meditation skill. Arguably the most important.</span></p><p><span>Anxiety? Why not do CBT or something. Tackle that </span><i><span>directly</span></i><span>, with whichever skills prove appropriate, and concrete feedback about whether it's working. How anxious </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> you, and is it more or less than one perceptual timestep ago? What is correlating with these fluctuations, and what does this imply about how to solve the problem?</span></p><p><span>Or better yet, why do you care about anxiety in the first place? What are you anxious </span><i><span>about</span></i><span> anyway?</span></p><p><span>Oh, public speaking? You're worried that you're gonna do a bad job, and this is pulling your attention away from where it needs to be in order to do a good job? Why not tackle </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> directly, with whichever skills prove appropriate? Skills like holding attention on the topic of your speech. Like awareness of when your attention is drifting towards unhelpful things, and of </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> your attention keeps getting dragged where it does. And whether there might be a reason that has elided your awareness.</span></p><p><span>Whatever the goal, there's always the option of intentionally practicing mindfulness towards the specific things needed to succeed at your goal. Instead of getting your koans from meditation teachers, you can notice the koans that nature puts between us and our goals. If you're diving for lobsters you might think the limiting skill is grabbing them before they get away, but the limit might actually be in your perceptual ontology. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qFTrfw9MMc3jT92Du/finding-the-seams-of-perception" rel="noreferrer"><span>Which one of these pieces of kelp — which is definitely kelp — is about to transform into a lobster?</span></a></p><p><span>To the extent that becomes limiting, lobster diving becomes a great way to build the skill of noticing the difference between our low level conclusions and the reality beneath. Would you notice if you were up against this limit? There's something to meditate on. Something that will keep your practice grounded in what you care about.</span></p><p><span>Without that, and without a skilled coach who can keep your efforts grounded </span><i><span>for</span></i><span> you, there's the risk of developing the meditation equivalent of "gym muscles". Of becoming someone who is </span><i><span>breath aware</span></i><span>, and who can let thoughts arise and pass "as thoughts" from a full lotus on the meditation cushion, but reverts to conflating their thoughts with reality under the pressure of thoughts </span><i><span>that matter, </span></i><span>and might even be right. Without any hard earned skills of coordinating thoughts in ways that stand up to pressure and serve to </span><i><span>shape</span></i><span> reality.</span></p><p><span>When the thought that arises is "Shit, I left the front door open. I don't know where my kid is", do you have to leave the meditative practice in order to act? Are you building skills for dealing with thoughts of the sort "We're on track to unacceptable outcomes" that </span><i><span>don't</span></i><span> require either giving up on meditation or giving up on reality?</span></p><p><span>This alternative is much harder. Try to do the thing you actually care about. Hone the skills of noticing </span><i><span>exactly</span></i><span> where you're going wrong. Develop awareness of where your perceptual ontology is failing you. And of where your awareness is failing you. Puzzles will multiply faster than the answers.</span></p><p><span>And as a result, things will stay interesting. It can be hard to stay motivated lifting weights up when they just end up back down in the same spot anyway. Or to focus on your breath when your breath isn't doing anything interesting, and you have to practice not-noticing the lack of a compelling answer to "Why am I doing this anyway? This is stupid".</span></p><p><span>It's a lot easier to exert yourself to maximum capacity when the connection is solid, and you know what you're fighting for. When there's a bull to restrain, or lobsters to catch. Or unacceptable outcomes on the horizon, and no clear way off the track.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/h99Wi5vfFbPasCFh5/farm-strength-vs-breath-awareness#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Selection for Selectability: Inductive Biases in Evolution and in Neural Networks](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JNp5FkYyDGBcfiY5B/selection-for-selectability-inductive-biases-in-evolution)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>This post was written as part of MATS 9.1 under the mentorship of Richard Ngo, and was written during Iliad Fellowship, to all of whom my thanks.</em></p>
<p><em>LLM Usage: prose drafted by Claude from my outline, talk materials, and notes.  I edited thereafter.  There is some residual Claude cringe in the more functional prose, but hopefully most of it is my own and the more entertaining for it.</em></p>
<h2>0.A. <strong>Precis</strong></h2>
<p>Evolution selects not only for having 'good genotype' but for having good genome architecture.  Over long timescales, selection reshapes genome architecture so that random mutations produce phenotypes which vary along directions of repeated environmental variation.  This <em>genome–environment alignment</em> is mathematically analogous to kernel alignment in neural networks.  The comparison rests not on the fatuous observation that both processes can be written as equations resembling gradient descent, but on shared structural motifs - many of the interesting things we've observed about, e.g. loss-landscape geometry, are adumbrated in biology.  This post draws the mathematical analogy and introduces the parallels I find most fun - genome–environment alignment ~ feature learning, the -matrix as, i.a., biology's very own measurement of low-rankness of finetuning, and neutral networks as the coolest example structure.</p>
<h2>0.B. <strong>Preface:</strong></h2>
<p>In <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Tm2dCH6dHE2ber53Y/coalitional-darwinism-and-the-instrumental-utility-of">Coalitional Darwinism</a> I argued that selection had a limited resolution and that therefore lineages should evolve to spend it well.  I have been working on a sequence of followups relating concepts from evolution to deep learning and modern LLM phenomena, but, my native disposition being an unwillingness to release anything less than the Magisterial Compendium of All Truths, I've had to force myself into writing small bits at a time.  Hoc vide.  This post will cover the minimum analogy.  If this analogy feels fatuous, please accept my assurances that it will cash out in the forthcoming 'applications' pieces.</p>
<p><strong>Tl;dr on the sequence as a whole:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Evolution is like SGD not just in mathematical form, but in the induced structural motifs.  It prefers flat minima, sorta-factored-solutions, redundant solutions, and lots of loose parameters for optimization.</li>
<li>Catalogue of structural motifs shared in ML and evolution: e.g., local-linearity / flatness, facilitated variation, error-correction, degeneracy and redundancy circuitry</li>
<li>Speculative applications of ideas derived from biology to machine learning, including the hydra effect and subliminal-learning/emergent-misalignment</li>
</ul>

<h2><strong>1.  A Population Is a Density Distribution in Genome Space</strong></h2>
<p>A genetics-deep-learning concordance:
An organism has a genome  → development turns the genome into a phenotype → the environment scores the phenotype.
An NN has parameters  → architecture turns parameters into behavior/outputs → loss scores the behavior.</p>
<p><img alt="Whiteboard: selection reweights a population distribution" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Bauyx5ApFuc2xfNho/p8jewmw6crlhbxdgb9da" /></p>
<p><em>One round of selection: the fitness landscape (left) reweights the current population distribution (right) into a new one (bottom) - probability mass flows toward fitter genotypes.</em></p>
<p>Selection reweights a population toward fitter phenotypes, just as SGD pushes weights towards lower-loss regions.</p>
<p>Population genetics is a bit of a hybrid between SGD and Bayesian learning - a population is best thought of as a distribution .  <em>Locally</em>  evolves as if you were doing Bayesian learning to optimize the fitness - it behaves like a fluid flowing down the loss valley.  Globally it behaves more like SGD, as it is highly localized in genotype space.  But bio navigates a super interesting tradeoff that we'll soon port to ML:  can only <em>move</em> in proportion to its <em>spread</em>, so there's a strong incentive to make sure organisms don't all Goodhart to the current environment and converge on a single locally optimal genotype.  This fluid ends up having a 'temperature' corresponding to mutations and other types of noise.  If you have some valley, the genome distribution will line up along the iso-loss curve, with some width set by this temperature.</p>
<p>Modulo the fact that SGD noise comes from qualitatively different sources and some of the associated phenomena are quite different, noise is noise and to zeroth order these are the same sort of thing.  If you know enough to be skeptical of this comparison, you know enough to figure what things should carry over or not.</p>
<h2><strong>2.  Evolution Learns by Aligning Mutations to Environmental Variation</strong></h2>
<p>That DNA works well when hit by random mutations is something that we have mostly forgotten to be surprised by.  It does a little bit of the CS Hamming-code-style error-correction but it's much more defense in depth.  It should naively be all the more surprising that it's possible to find beneficial mutations at all.  There is a deep force at work ensuring that mutations are always <em>usefully exploring</em> phenotype space.</p>
<p>We'll abstract away from the molecular specifics and instead imagine an abstract <strong>genotype–phenotype map</strong>: the developmental process that turns genetic changes into phenotypic ones - .  What's important is that the map  is itself encoded by the genome, and so selection acts on it.</p>
<p><img alt="Whiteboard: genome–environment alignment" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Bauyx5ApFuc2xfNho/u8ivebfmlouoqzud66yk" /></p>
<p><em>Top: if the environment changes once, adaptation is just setting genes to the right values.  Bottom: if it oscillates between demanding "tall-happy" and "short-sad," a genome that flips between those combinations in one mutation out-adapts one that needs to grope haplessly after the right code.  The genome's structure - not just its current phenotype - is under selection.</em></p>
<p>Suppose the environment oscillates between demanding one combination of traits and another - on the whiteboard above, "tall and happy" versus "short and sad" (a stipulated toy).  If the environment changed once and settled, there'd be nothing interesting to say: set the genes to the right values and be done.  But under oscillation, a lineage whose genome happens to be organized so that a single common mutation flips it between the two demanded combinations re-adapts faster after each swing.  A lineage that needs three independent rare mutations, in the right order will, eventually, get yeehawed into the dustbin of history.  Zoom out enough that you don't see the oscillations, and just see the average - the more selectable lineage will, ceteris paribus, be fitter.</p>
<p>How big is the advantage?  Let the environment oscillate over a timescale , and let lineage 1 adapt faster than lineage 2, so that it is well-adapted for a time  longer than lineage 2 each cycle.  With fitness depression  during adaptation (<em>between</em> the lineages - we only track <em>relative</em> fitness), the more adaptable lineage gets an average relative-fitness increment .  The small parameter here is , so this analysis makes most sense where oscillations are reasonably rare but the fitness-at-stake relatively large.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>This is what I mean by <strong>selection for selectability</strong>: the architecture of variation is shaped by which variation selection was able to see and use.  Foreshadowing, we will call this <strong>genome–environment alignment</strong> - the genome structures itself so that common mutations align with common environmental variations.</p>
<h2><strong>3.  Feature Learning Is Genome–Environment Alignment</strong></h2>
<p>At any moment in training, an NN has a well-defined 'reservoir of variation' on which it can draw for the next gradient step.  Consider some circuit which can be implemented either as robust  or as fragile .  Perturbations to  break it - on the one hand it is well-protected from competition, but on the other, it is well-protected from competition.   performs the same function but is stable under many perturbations - a network with  will be much more adaptable than one with  - it has more effective parameters (at least assuming the two circuits <em>require</em> the same number of parameters).</p>
<h3><strong>3.A.  The eNTK Tells us a Network's Directions of Preferred Variation</strong></h3>
<p>Let's introduce the <strong>empirical neural tangent kernel</strong> (eNTK).  Write the network's behavior as ; a small parameter change translates to behavior via the Jacobian,</p>
<p></p>
<p>just as a mutation effects the phenotype through the genotype–phenotype map, .  The eNTK comes in two flavours.  The data-space version measures how similar two inputs look to the network's gradients,</p>
<p></p>
<p>so that under a gradient step, whatever the network learns about  spills over onto  in proportion to .  The parameter-space (dual) version instead scores how similar two proposed parameter changes  are as seen by behavior.  I typically don't distinguish between them unless it's necessary; note that the parameter-space version is, for a model which outputs logits, the Fisher Information matrix.  Importantly, it depends only on the network, not on the loss.  Don't let me hear you saying circuits are eigen-anything of the loss-Hessian...  No defeatism!  Wir müssen wissen!</p>
<p>The parameter-space eNTK is essentially the network's 'first order inductive bias' - the directions in weight-space it is most prepared to move.  The genome's architecture (which here for concreteness we pretend is identical to the Jacobian) is likewise a lineage's inductive bias in the same sense.</p>
<h3><strong>3.B.  Kernel Learning Fits; Feature Learning Rotates</strong></h3>
<p><img alt="Whiteboard: the kernel/antenna picture" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Bauyx5ApFuc2xfNho/ahy1lphih0arn2cbkbor" /></p>
<p><em>Top: Correspondence between NNs and genetics.  The local linearization is the kernel in either case.  Bottom: The antenna analogy: kernel learning fits eigenvalues to a fixed set of eigenvectors; feature learning rotates the eigenvectors 'towards the data'.</em></p>
<p>The eNTK and the GP map both have a bunch of eigenvalues and a bunch of eigenvectors.  Think of these as a set of antennae, listening for 'signal' from function space.</p>
<p>There are then two very different modes of learning.  In the <strong>"lazy" or kernel regime</strong>, the antennae stay fixed and training only adjusts how much of each direction gets used - which is just selection acting on <em>standing variation</em>, reweighting options the system already offers.  Kernel learning is well thought of as advanced linear regression.  In a meaningful sense which I will make precise in a later post on 'inductive bias', kernel learning really doesn't 'learn' anything - it never updates its inductive biases, never builds a world model etc.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>'Real' learning is when the kernel <em>moves</em> during training - somewhat-annoyingly-to-me this is called <strong>feature learning</strong> in the learning theory literature.  Think of the antennae rotating 'towards the data' - it fits the functions its fitting (and they're fit to yet more fit functions dontcha know).</p>
<p>Irl the antenna metaphor is about eigenstuff - eigenvectors of  are the antennae, the eigenvalues their sensitivities.  Lazy/kernel learning holds the vectors fixed, while in feature learning, the Jacobian, and thus the eigenvectors move.  Genome–environment alignment is the same statement about : recurrent selection rotates its leading eigendirections toward the combinations of traits the environment repeatedly demands.</p>
<p>But here's where it comes together!  This is 'the same' as evolution reshaping the genotype–phenotype map.  Kernel learning ≈ fast selection on standing variation; feature learning ≈ aligning your variation-generator with the structure of the environment.  (Though note that whether this fast-slow decomposition holds depends, iiuc, on the training regime?)</p>
<h3><strong>3.C.  Selection and SGD Obey the Same Evolution Equations in the Kernel Regime</strong></h3>
<p>The response equations make this exact.  Quantitative genetics summarizes selection response as Lande's equation:</p>
<p></p>
<p>the change in mean traits being the selection gradient  pushed through , the covariance of heritable variation.  Of this G-matrix we shall say much.  Kernel-regime training obeys</p>
<p></p>
<p>the change in behavior being the output-space loss gradient  filtered through the kernel .  The two objects occupy the same slot in the same first-order equation: each filters an external pressure through the variation the system can currently express, and - this matters - neither knows anything about the loss or fitness function.  Structure-to-function is firewalled from function-to-utility on both sides.</p>
<p>By saying these are 'the same' I mean "weak evidence because they can be written as schematically similar equations" + "moderate evidence coming from shared surprising phenomena" (of which I will summarize one cool one in this post, but a ton in a later one, so please withhold judgement).  Note though that the comparison that works is between the <em>effects of perturbations</em> - what a mutation does to the phenotype, what a parameter delta does to behavior.  Shared form is not shared dynamics; the rest of the sequence is about which further parallels are real.</p>
<h2><strong>4.  The G-Matrix Measures Accessible Variations, for Finches as for Claude</strong></h2>
<p>Biologists don't have access to the GP map; instead they focus on a few high-level phenotypic traits (like height, pelt thickness, beak width, etc) and measure how they covary.  The relevant quantity is the <strong>-matrix</strong>, the covariance matrix of heritable variation in the chosen traits.  Diagonal entries say how much each trait can respond to selection; off-diagonals say which traits respond will get pulled along for the ride.</p>
<p>The -matrix is the biological half of the response-equation pair from §3.C: selection pushes with a gradient , and  says  decides what actually moves.  Perhaps unsurprisingly given our definitions, populations tend to evolve along <a href="https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1558-5646.1996.tb03563.x">"genetic lines of least resistance"</a>.  And measured -matrices are typically <a href="https://doi.org/10.1534/genetics.105.052407">low-rank</a>: most of the available variation lives in a small number of effective directions.  Note the similarity to the low intrinsic dimensionality of fine-tuning, LoRA, and friends...</p>
<p><img alt="Intrinsic dimension of fine-tuning falls with model size" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Bauyx5ApFuc2xfNho/zbdtkm4noecoqhgjxwuw" /></p>
<p><em>Reproduced from Figure 3 of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.13255">Aghajanyan, Zettlemoyer &amp; Gupta (2020)</a>: the intrinsic dimension of fine-tuning (, the number of free parameters needed in a random subspace to reach 90% of full fine-tuning performance on MRPC) falls as pre-trained models grow.  This is the ML version of "low-rank  matrix" - relevant macrobehaviours tend to be pretty accessible and rather correlated overall.</em></p>
<h3><strong>4.A.  LLM Cross-Labilities Can be Likewise Measured by a G-Matrix</strong></h3>
<p>(Presumably someone has done this for various LLM traits, but idk)</p>
<p>Consider traits of an LLM that you can score with some behavioural losses , e.g., alignment evals, coding evals, talk-like-a-pirate-evals.  Each score has a gradient with respect to parameters; then we can ask how much each behaviour wants to talk to the same parameters.  The overlaps form a trait-level -matrix for the model, and the off-diagonals are <strong>cross-labilities</strong>: when training yanks trait A, how much trait B gets dragged along.  For concreteness, this might look like:</p>
<p></p>
<p>(This would be a funny world to live in wouldn't it... imagine the ICLR paper titles...)</p>
<h3><strong>4.B.  The eeNTK Is the Trait-Level G-Matrix</strong></h3>
<p>If each trait  is scored by a loss  on the model's outputs, the "G-matrix" analogue for NNs is</p>
<p></p>
<p>the network's kernel  sandwiched between the traits' output-gradients.  I call this the <strong>extended eNTK (eeNTK)</strong>.</p>
<p>I'll save it for the followup speculation post, but I expect this sort of trait correlation to be half of an explanation of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424">emergent misalignment</a>.</p>
<h2><strong>5.  Neutral Networks Are the Flagship Parallel</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>5.A.  Populations Bank Cryptic Variation in Neutral Networks</strong></h3>
<p>(Note the T!  neu_T_ral.  Not my fault, sorry.)</p>
<p>Most mutations do approximately nothing.  The genotype–phenotype map is very very many-to-one: the set of genotypes producing a given phenotype - its <strong>neutral network</strong> - is generically enormous, and typically very space-filling.  Locally, these are the directions  with .  A cool example: in RNA structure, they can roughly <a href="https://royalsocietypublishing.org/doi/10.1098/rspb.1994.0040">compute this</a>.  Claude also points to '<a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-022-32538-z">direct experimental support</a>' for this from high-throughput ribozyme screens.</p>
<p><img alt="Whiteboard: neutral networks facilitate adaptation" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Bauyx5ApFuc2xfNho/tp9bdwah2uvujc4luwd2" /></p>
<p><em>Top: a population that can't climb a fitness barrier can, with more dimensions to move in, go around it.  Bottom: in "peacetime" the population spreads around the valley like water; when the environment changes, part of the spread population is already near the new optimum.</em></p>
<p>For population distributions, the population's density in genome-space doesn't concentrate at a single point, but spreads out along the iso-loss set - in particular it <a href="https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.96.17.9716">concentrates in the densest, most robust regions</a> of the network.  The spread carries <strong>cryptic variation</strong> - genetic differences with no visible effect today, which nonetheless change what tomorrow's mutations do.  A population pre-spread along a neutral set has already made surface area with tons of directions a new environment might demand, and likewise can find route-around barriers it could never climb <em>over</em>.  Another Claude-suggested reference: "<a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/nature10083">in-vitro RNA-enzyme experiments</a>, populations that had quietly accumulated cryptic diversity under purifying selection adapted several-fold faster when handed a new substrate."</p>
<h3><strong>5.B.  Hessian Eigenvalues Mirror Mutation Effects</strong></h3>
<p>The ML analogue of this is the Hessian spectrum - the curvature of the loss-landscape.  Measured Hessian spectra of trained networks have the same characteristic form - a <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.04454"> bulk</a> of small eigenvalues, plus a <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1811.07062">few large outliers</a>.  Relatedly, independently trained minima are typically joined by <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.10026">low-loss paths</a> - <em>mode connectivity</em>... Nice, yeah?</p>
<h3><strong>5.C.  Flatness Counteracts Noise</strong></h3>
<p>Why should optimization <em>produce</em> this geometry?  Ecce the <em>noise floor</em> from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Tm2dCH6dHE2ber53Y/coalitional-darwinism-and-the-instrumental-utility-of">Coalitional Darwinism</a>:  selection cannot see fitness differences below roughly ; SGD cannot see loss differences below its own noise scale.  A direction whose curvature sits below the floor is effectively neutral: nothing constrains it, so its eigenvalue is not being optimized - its motion is random.  The system therefore drifts through these invisible directions until it happens into a region of sufficient flatness.  Flat regions are where drift comes to rest.  This is a super well-known thing in ML but I think there are a lot of insightful angles on it and I'll reserve this discussion for a later post.</p>
<h2><strong>6.  Next Time:</strong></h2>
<p>The idea behind this sequence of posts is that evolution and SGD have common structural motifs.  I don't care - and probably you shouldn't either - that we can make enough assumptions to have them obey the same equation.  The point is instead that they both exhibit shared phenomena - not as modelling artefacts, but as  apparently convergent properties of solutions to massive optimization problems.  I think it's important to understand these, first because these convergent structures possibly make a form of sparsity/interpretability a convergent property of the solutions to either.  Second though, is the converse - that if we observe something interesting in SGD <em>and</em> evolution, then it cannot tell us anything about the idiosyncratic properties of SGD, neural networks, data structure, or cognition.</p>
<p>Later posts will talk in <em>much</em> greater depth about analogies between bio and ML.  After that, I'll take you to the speculators' corner for some speculating on whether common ML phenomena are usefully understood as paralleling phenomena from evolution.</p>
<p>A few fun ones:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Additivity and averaging.</strong>  Genomes are selected to keep mutations useful across genetic backgrounds - recombination demands it.  The fact that checkpoint averaging and model soups work at all looks like the response to a similar demand.  This is, I think, a lot deeper than it sounds, and relates to plasticity, noise robustness, steering vectors etc.</li>
<li><strong>The Hydra effect.</strong>  Ablate an attention head and downstream circuitry compensates.  Biology's degeneracy and regulatory buffering suggest how this kind of self-repair arises from optimization under noise, rather than being engineered.</li>
<li><strong>Selective sweeps.</strong>  Subliminal learning and emergent misalignment look like a sweep dragging linked variation along with it - hitchhiking in trait space.</li>
<li><strong>Facilitated variation.</strong>  Conserved core machinery plus cheap regulatory knobs, and whether late training increasingly adjusts routing over reusable circuits rather than the circuits themselves.  This suggests LLMs might be 'a few core circuits plus a ton of circumstantial regulators and error-correctors' - I'm thinkin' on just what this should look like still.</li>
</ul>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-1"><p>Mathematically, you can understand this from the perspective of secular perturbation theory.  This is at least sometimes a useful mathematical tool in ML, but I haven't fully thought through a general theory.  For a good, if non-biological, example, see <a href="https://centralflows.github.io/">'central flows'</a>.  I'd be curious if one could somehow think of batch noise this way, but seems dubious to me. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-2"><p>Tldr, you can write a nonlinear kernel evolution problem as a hierarchy of linear kernel problems - feature-functions are fit to meta-features, meta-features to meta-meta-features, etc.  Skipping details, on a new observation, you update your inductive bias on how to update your inductive bias on...  Roberts and Yaida's PDLT book does this perturbatively for MLPs. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-JDTbtgrKW3onoqjKs-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JNp5FkYyDGBcfiY5B/selection-for-selectability-inductive-biases-in-evolution#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [When is Unlimited Optimization Catastrophic?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4JCne6evQjtjxXKED/when-is-unlimited-optimization-catastrophic)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>This post discusses research I've completed along with my colleagues </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/leo-cymbalista" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Leo Cymbalista</span></i></a><i><span>, </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/alfred-harwood" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Alfred Harwood</span></i></a><i><span>, and </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/josefaustino" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Jose Faustino</span></i></a><i><span> at </span></i><a href="https://dovetailresearch.org/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Dovetail Research</span></i></a><i><span>. Most of the ideas in this post are expanded upon in our paper which can be found on </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28881" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>arXiv</span></i></a><i><span>. This work was funded by the </span></i><a href="https://aria.org.uk/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Advanced Research + Invention Agency (ARIA)</span></i></a><i><span> through project code MSAI-SE01-P005.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><p><span>A common justification for the danger of AI comes from the idea that human value is fragile. That is, if we modify our values and heavily optimize the world for the modification, we are likely to end up in a valueless world. In the LessWrong post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GNnHHmm8EzePmKzPk/value-is-fragile" rel="noreferrer"><span>Value is Fragile</span></a><span> which canonicalizes this idea, Eliezer Yudkowsky gives several examples where "forgetting" to specify a dimension of human value such as consciousness or boredom to a powerful AI can intuitively result in an undesirable outcome that is endlessly repetitive or meaningless respectively. While his examples in the post all take this form, he argues more generally that any future not shaped with reliable inheritance from human values will contain almost nothing of worth. This idea is especially concerning in the midst of current-day AIs aligned through one-time techniques such as RLHF before being deployed with no farther realignment. As we continue to create stronger AIs, we must have a formal understanding of this fragility in order to safely avoid a valueless future.</span></p><p><span>To study this problem, we model one-time alignment techniques which provide a similarity guarantee between the human and agent value functions, which we call an alignment test or a proxy condition. Our results provide conditions on the strictness of these guarantees and the true value function under which an agent with a catastrophic value function can make it through training and go on to heavily optimize the world.</span></p><p><span>This post is an informal presentation of the work in our </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28881" rel="noreferrer"><span>paper</span></a><span>. Full proofs and smaller details have been omitted. Readers interested in those details are encouraged to read the paper.</span></p><h1><span>A Model of Alignment</span></h1><p><span>Imagine a group of human researchers who are training a powerful AI agent. Their goal is to have the agent optimize the world in a way that increases human value. Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> denote the human value function where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the set of world states so that humans assign some value between 0 and 1 to every state.</span></p><p><span>In training the agent, it gains its own value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The researchers cannot guarantee that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are identical, but through alignment training, they can ensure that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> will satisfy a proxy condition that makes it similar to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. After training, the agent is deployed to optimize the world. We model optimization as the agent altering the relative probability of world states to prioritize states that it values highly. Mathematically, we capture this notion with the definition of an optimizer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> below.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 3.1</span></b><span> An </span><b><span>Optimizer</span></b><span> is a map </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that sends the optimizing power </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, a Borel measurable target value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and an initial prior over the world states </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to a probability distribution over the set of world states that satisfies the following properties:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>With no optimizing power, the distribution is unchanged:</span></li></ul><div></div><ul><li value="1"><span>The expected value of the target is monotonically increasing with optimizing power:</span></li></ul><div></div><ul><li value="1"><span>As optimizing power increases to infinity, probability becomes concentrated in areas where the target is large:</span></li></ul><div></div><ul><li value="1"><span>As optimizing power increases to infinity, the expectation of the target approaches its highest value within the world states:</span></li></ul><div></div></blockquote><p><span>We are particularly concerned about the limit of optimizing power in order to model the danger of agents that exhibit recursive self-improvement. One example of an optimizer is the Boltzmann optimizer defined by the equation</span></p><div></div><p><span>which smoothly transfers more and more probability into higher-valued states. Below is an image which shows how a distribution over four states changes under the influence of a Boltzmann optimizer.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="boltzmannFiniteDiagram-1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787343031/lexical_client_uploads/snksckk99q4wbw5etpos.png" /><figcaption><p><span>A sequence of graphs showing a distribution induced by a Boltzmann Optimizer. </span><b><span>Graph 1:</span></b><span> A graph displaying the value of four states A, B, C, and D with respect to the target value function. </span><b><span>Graph 2:</span></b><span> A graph displaying the initial probability distribution which is also the distribution induced when optimizing power is zero. </span><b><span>Graph 3:</span></b><span> A graph displaying the induced distribution when optimizing power is 1. Higher-valued states C and D see an increase in probability while lower-valued states see a decrease. </span><b><span>Graph 4:</span></b><span> A graph displaying the induced distribution when optimizing power is 10. Now that optimizing power is large enough, the only state that is seeing increases in probability is the highest-valued state D which has become much more likely than the other states.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We classify an agent value function as partially catastrophic if it eventually takes and keeps the expectation of human value below a given point indefinitely as optimizing power increases. This is captured in the following definition.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 3.2 </span></b><span>Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be a real number such that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. A value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><b><span>-catastrophic</span></b><span> for a value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> when for all initial priors </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and for all optimizers </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><div></div><p><span>In the extreme case that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-catastrophic for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, we say that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is </span><b><span>catastrophic</span></b><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The quantity </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> represents an upper bound on the expectation of human value in the limit of optimization. The above definition requires not just that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> takes human value below </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, but that it does so for all optimizers. This makes it a strong definition of catastrophe as some functions will take human value to different places depending on the optimizer. In our fragility results, this can be interpreted as modeling the humans as having the ability to guide the agent in what optimizer it uses to affect the world which should, in principle, favor the researchers.</span></p><p><span>With these two definitions in hand, we want to look at a few alignment tests that restrict what functions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can come out of training to understand what proxy conditions admit partially catastrophic value functions.</span></p><h2><span>Alignment Tests</span></h2><p><span>In addition to different alignment tests, we also vary the worlds and the set of valid value functions. Put together, we call each test and world setup a framework. In this section, we go through the three proxy condition definitions. In the next section, we give the results for each of the frameworks.</span></p><h3><span>Finite Framework</span></h3><p><span>Our simplest test takes places in a finite world — that is, one where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a finite set. All value functions of the form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are valid. If humans assign value 1 to a state, then we can understand the humans valuing this state as "perfect," though it may be the case that humans do not value any state as perfect.</span></p><p><span>Here, the researchers manage to bound the disagreement rate between </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> below a fixed value </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> over a uniform distribution of the world states. In symbols, all value functions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that result from training must satisfy the following definition.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 4.1 </span></b><span>A function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is an </span><b><span>F-proxy</span></b><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> when </span></p><div></div><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> represents the uniform distribution over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The value </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> in the proxy condition quantifies the amount of misspecification in the researchers' representation of human values with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> representing a perfect specification.</span></p><h3><span>Continuous Framework</span></h3><p><span>Our second test takes place in a continuous and bounded world. The test itself takes a similar form to the test in the finite framework with a few key differences. We model the world as being of the form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> so that we can effectively model a world defined by the positions of a fixed number of atoms in a bounded space. We additionally require that all value functions take a more specific form defined by membership in the set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> defined as</span></p><div></div><p><span>This restriction effectively pulls a canonical representative from every Von Neumann-Morgenstern equivalence class of continuous bounded nonconstant value functions. The researchers have managed to bound the probability that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> disagree by more than a set tolerance </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> over a sampling distribution of world states </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. In symbols, all agent value functions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> resulting from training must satisfy the following definition.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 5.1 </span></b><span>A function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a </span><b><span>C-proxy</span></b><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> when</span></p><div></div></blockquote><h3><span>Attributes Framework</span></h3><p><span>Our final test takes a significantly different form from the other two and applies to a wide class of worlds. We take inspiration from and alter the setup put forth in similar work done by Zhuang and Hadfield-Menell in their NeurIPS paper titled </span><a href="https://www.google.com/url?sa=t&amp;source=web&amp;rct=j&amp;opi=89978449&amp;url=https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2020/file/b607ba543ad05417b8507ee86c54fcb7-Paper.pdf&amp;ved=2ahUKEwjvjMqGhbKWAxXmiisGHVh_FkIQFnoECCQQAQ&amp;usg=AOvVaw1w99DF6jz9FgW3-Eh-BFFa" rel="noreferrer"><span>Consequences of Misaligned AI</span></a><span> where, much like the arguments given by Yudkowsky, human value is understood as being made up of a finite list of attributes. Each attribute </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> acts like a value function on its own which measures the "amount" of a given aspect of each world state. Human value is then represented as a function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of the form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a continuous strictly increasing function that maps the attribute state </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to one combined value.</span></p><p><span>Some typical things we might think of as attributes could be abstract concepts "happiness" or more concrete concepts like the "number of cats." Another way to split up human value could be for each attribute to represent the value function of an individual human. For a single value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> there can be many ways to split it up into a set of attributes that fully determine it with some function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Attributes may trade off against each other due to the dynamics of the world. For example, increasing one human's value may decrease the value of their arch nemesis. So, we define the feasible attribute states </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> as the combinations of attributes which are attained by some world state </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. As a result, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is exactly the range of the function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Whatever split into attributes is used, the researchers have designed the training process to guarantee that the agent's value function will be strictly increasing with all of the same attributes. In symbols, all agent value functions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> resulting from training must satisfy the following definition.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 6.1 </span></b><span>A value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a </span><b><span>tradeoff proxy</span></b><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> when there is a function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that is continuous over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and is strictly increasing such that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for all </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Unlike in Consequences of Misaligned AI and Yudkowsky's examples, we are not interested in "subset proxies" defined as strictly increasing on a strict subset of the attributes. Instead, we are interested in when fully specifying every attribute can still result in partial catastrophe.</span></p><p><span>These are the three specific proxy conditions we considered in our paper. There are certainly others that can be studied, and a more general analysis of a wide range of proxy conditions is discussed in the future work section, but these three serve as good common sense alignment techniques that we can analyze. Now, we go through each one and find conditions on the parameters of the test and the shape of the human value function under which a partially catastrophic value function passes the test.</span></p><h1><span>Results</span></h1><h2><span>Finite Framework</span></h2><p><span>In the finite framework, we have the following result classifying when a fully catastrophic F-proxy exists for nontrivial human value functions.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Theorem 4.2 </span></b><span>Define </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> take at least three distinct values over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>There exists a catastrophic F-proxy for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if and only if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Theorem 4.2 tells us that the more states the humans value as perfect, the less strict the researchers must be to filter out catastrophic functions. This makes intuitive sense since the more states humans value highly, the less picky they will be about the state they end up in in general. Notably, this result does not say that a full specification is needed to filter out catastrophe. This is partially due to the strength of our definition of catastrophe: a function is only </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-catastrophic if every optimizer takes human value below </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. For any amount of misspecification, there are many F-proxies that take human value down for some but not all optimizers.</span></p><h3><span>Example</span></h3><p><span>An agent has been put in charge of the interlockings at a railroad. Consider the situation pictured in the image and table below depicting two trains coming to a junction with two switches and the values assigned to different states of the switches given by the humans and an agent. The humans would most prefer that both trains be allowed to continue straight (aa). Unnecessary turns will slow down the trains and so are less valuable (ab and ba). If both trains turn, this will result in a crash which the humans assign a very low value (bb). If the proxy condition for this agent has </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (allowing just one state of disagreement), then it's possible that the agent values the crash state very highly, making it catastrophic.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 75.2239990234375%;"><img alt="finiteExampleTrains-1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787328581/lexical_client_uploads/helmnmes1bel3m1oonlz.png" /><figcaption><p><span>A diagram showing a railroad junction. Two trains are traveling to the right and the agent can control two switches to alter their courses. There are four states the agent can leave the railroad in: both switches set to straight (aa), train 2 made to turn only (ab), train 1 made to turn only (ba), and both trains made to turn (bb).</span></p></figcaption></figure><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>State</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Human Value</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Agent Value (Catastrophic)</span></p></th></tr><tr style="height: 50px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span>aa</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.9</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.9</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 50px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span>ab</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.7</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.7</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>bb</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>1.0</span></b></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span>Continuous Framework</span></h2><p><span>We have the following result that applies to any sampling probability density function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Theorem 5.1</span></b><span> For all </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, for all </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, there exists a catastrophic C-proxy for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The rough idea behind the construction of a catastrophic C-proxy </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is to alter </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> by adding a thin spike at minimizer of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (so </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) to make </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and an equalizing spike somewhere else so that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a member of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. With this modification (along with some other changes that are omitted from this sketch), the spikes can be made thin enough to satisfy the proxy condition. Since </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> has a unique maximizer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, optimizing for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> will eventually concentrate probability around </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> which takes the expectation of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to 0, making </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> a catastrophic proxy.</span></p><p><span>Theorem 5.1 tells us that any misspecification in the proxy condition, no matter the value function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, admits a catastrophic proxy (and thus </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-catastrophic proxies as well). While this result says that a total safety guarantee using this alignment test is impossible, it's worth noting that the catastrophic C-proxy constructed in the proof is not particularly natural. Our result says nothing about how likely catastrophic or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-catastrophic proxies are to occur from the training process — only that they exist. Estimates for such probabilities would provide a stronger rigorous justification for fears about fragility and is discussed in the future work section of this post.</span></p><h3><span>Example</span></h3><p><span>In the images below, we depict the human and agent value functions in a world defined by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and the resulting distributions from optimizing for each. The proxy function mirrors the construction described in the sketch of the proof and has a unique maximizer at a minimizer of the human value function and is catastrophic. </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.72%;"><img alt="continuousExampleValueFunctions-1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787331124/lexical_client_uploads/sitiarsezxh4ffrtmvi9.png" /><figcaption><p><span>An image showing the graph of two value functions over the set of world states [0,1]. </span><b><span>Left:</span></b><span> A graph displaying the true/human value function graphed as a blue line. </span><b><span>Right:</span></b><span> A graph displaying the proxy value function graphed as a black line. This proxy is catastrophic since its unique highest-valued state is exactly the lowest-valued state of the true value function.</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="continuousExampleGrid-1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787330996/lexical_client_uploads/qxlvphxzsygjddgqsadb.png" /><figcaption><p><span>A grid showing the effect of a Boltzmann optimizer on a distribution over the states. In the background of each graph is the true value function shown in blue. Graphs in the top row show the distribution (scaled to fit the box) in green when the target is the true value function while the bottom row graphs show the distribution in orange when the target is the proxy value function. Graphs in the same row show the distribution at the same optimizing power </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. When there is lots of optimizing power, the distributions become extremely concentrated.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Attributes Framework</span></h2><p><span>To state the main result of this framework, we must introduce a couple additional definitions and notation. We have the following partial ordering defined over the attribute-space </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (i.e. vectors where each component represents the value of one attribute).</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 6.2</span></b><span> Define the relation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for any two elements </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> as</span></p><div></div><p><span>Additionally,</span><b><span> </span></b><span class="math-tex"></span></p></blockquote><p><span>The relation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, which we read as "precedes" (or "succeeds" for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) has the property that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> implies </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for all strictly increasing functions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. We use this relation to define a set of attribute states of interest below.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Definition 6.3 </span></b><span>Let the feasible states </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be a bounded set. The </span><b><span>optimizing states</span></b><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are defined </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p></blockquote><p><span>The optimizing states can be thought of as the Pareto frontier of the attribute space. From each member of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, you cannot increase one attribute without decreasing another or leaving </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. We can now state the primary result.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Theorem 6.1</span></b><span> Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be bounded and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. There is an </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-catastrophic tradeoff proxy for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if and only if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>While all tradeoff proxies are strictly increasing in the attributes that humans care about, the problem comes from the fact that the proxies can tradeoff between attributes at different rates. For example, if there are two attributes </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and the human value function is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> while </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, then we can expect that, depending on the shape of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> will optimize towards states that are larger in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> while the humans will prefer states that are larger in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Theorem 6.1 confirms this intuition. In the proof, we show that every optimizing point is the unique maximizer for some function of the form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and thus, the worst case proxy could take value down to the lowest human value along the optimizing points.</span></p><h3><span>Example</span></h3><p><span>Consider a restaurant agent tasked with handling a budget to purchase ingredients and prepare meals. The agent has been trained to care about two attributes: the number of meals served and overall customer satisfaction</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmlh18dolvta"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmlh18dolvta">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. The tradeoff here is in the quality of ingredients the agent decides to buy. If the agent wants higher satisfaction, it will purchase more expensive ingredients to make a smaller number of higher-quality meals.</span></p><p><span>A graph of the feasible region is given below. Every point on the curved edge of the feasible region is an optimizing point. According to our theorem, the lowest-valued optimizing point will be the highest-valued point for some tradeoff proxy. Perhaps surprisingly, this includes the point on the far right of the figure where customer satisfaction is completely sacrificed for meals served. If the humans were hoping for the agent to compromise between the attributes they trained it to care about, they may be in for a bad surprise.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 66.46399902343751%;"><img alt="attributeExampleFeasibleRegion-1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787336189/lexical_client_uploads/p0vjtjjilktrjlr642up.png" /><figcaption><p><span>A diagram showing a feasible region for two attributes. The feasible region is shaded red showing the physically possible combinations of meals prepared and customer satisfaction. Shown as blue lines are the level curves of a strictly increasing function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Despite increasing with both attributes, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>'s highest-value point within the feasible region is on the bottom-right where customer satisfaction is completely sacrificed to optimize meals served.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Discussion</span></h1><p><span>We don't expect that the model we've presented of the alignment problem is perfectly representative of the challenges that humanity faces today, but the general negative flavor of our results signals that achieving safety guarantees when developing superintelligence will be a significant undertaking.</span></p><p><span>Our work highlights the dangers of overoptimization. Since even idealized alignment guarantees do not remove the possibility of catastrophe, our work suggests that developing agents with the goal of unimpeded optimization is generally unsafe. One possible alternative could be to develop new learning designs which have a consistent connection to humans such as quantilizers. We hope that by developing a more formal understanding of value fragility, we can guide AI development towards safer techniques that robustly prevent the possibility — however small — of a valueless future.</span></p><h2><span>Future Work</span></h2><p><span>One limitation of this work is that our results are purely about the existence of catastrophic outcomes — not how likely they are. An optimistic reading of our results can argue that our constructions are unnatural and thus not likely to come about as a result of training. A major difficulty with modeling and calculating the probability of catastrophe is making a choice of prior over proxy functions. An obvious general choice is to use a simplicity prior which values simpler functions as more likely. However, a simplicity prior is determined by a universal Turing machine and, as it turns out, this choice can make the probability of catastrophe arbitrarily large or small. In the near future, I plan to write a post detailing this difficulty in more detail. One way forward could be to make a specific choice of UTM whose simplicity bias lines up with the inductive bias of some real-world learning algorithm.</span></p><p><span>Another limitation is that our analysis was only completed for three classes of proxy conditions when many more exist. Future work could look at lots of proxy conditions (for example, those defined by metrics on the value functions) and complete a more all-encompassing analysis. An important note is that some proxy conditions, like the condition </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, actually do filter out the most severe partially catastrophic value functions as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is made smaller. However, we'd argue that this condition (and we suspect all conditions that give useful guarantees in general) is not feasibly implemented in some sense. In particular, the sup-metric is a global property whose value can change drastically for localized changes to the values of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, so it seems infeasible to guarantee this condition everywhere.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmlh18dolvta"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmlh18dolvta">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is a simplification for the sake of the example. Realistically, this is far from a full specification of human values. For example, an agent trained on just these two attributes may decide to fund a robbery of a more wealthy restaurant which goes against human values not captured by these two attributes.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4JCne6evQjtjxXKED/when-is-unlimited-optimization-catastrophic#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI #182: Pause For Reflection](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JSZkzsi8cD4pW6ffA/ai-182-pause-for-reflection)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This was a week of quiet aftermath, an opportunity to process recent events and start to figure out the path forward.</p>
<p>OpenAI is attempting to turn its ship around. Investors are questioning the turnover in its C-suite, but the bigger problems are in alignment, infrastructure and supervision, and in its training pipeline. OpenAI has now taken <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/openai-takes-initial-steps-to-address?r=67wny"><strong>initial steps to address What Happened leading up to HuggingFace attack</strong></a>, including pauses to development while new safeguards are put in place and problems are diagnosed. These are promising early signs, but it is early. We will see if they follow through, and we still await the post-mortem of the HuggingFace attack.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25540"></span>


</div>
<p>Anthropic revenue continues to climb as they prepare for their IPO, although growth has slowed somewhat recently. However, they too have plenty of problems under the hood. They shared many of them in <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/anthropic-risk-report-august-2026?r=67wny"><strong>the August 2026 Anthropic Risk Report</strong></a>.</p>
<p>This week also offered time to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/on-dwarkesh-patels-podcast-with-ryan?r=67wny"><strong>cover Dwarkesh Patel’s Podcast With Ryan Greenblatt</strong></a>, centrally on the potential for AI recursive self-improvement. I am working on a follow-up post to some other issues raised during that podcast.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>

</ol>


<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>The <a href="https://openai.com/index/how-enterprises-put-ai-to-work/">enterprises that embrace AI and use OpenAI services more often</a>, what they call ‘frontier firms,’ keep rapidly using more AI, whereas use by typical firms is growing a lot more slowly.</p>
<p>It is weird the gap used to be so small.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/JSZkzsi8cD4pW6ffA/d1jokgeoj6h5hgettveu" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>They also more often use advanced capabilities like Plugins and skills, as you would expect. Agentic use now has risen to 64% of all OpenAI tokens, up from almost none a year ago.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/martin_casado/status/2087780735403089967">Navigate the old JRPG Phantasy Star via direct ROM probe</a>, since that is easier than using the screen, although it is also cheating. I should play that one at some point, I really enjoyed PS2, PS3 and PS4 but never had a Sega Master System.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Seeing_Redlines/status/2089382498149237063">AI doesn’t get you around things like discrimination lawsuits,</a> if your instructions clearly discriminate or the results involve clear statistical discrimination. It also seems it can’t write LinkedIn-style posts that fool me or Pangram.</p>
<p>LLMs are not responsible for the best news of the week, <a href="https://x.com/abuchanlife/status/2090227492229722118">that Moderna’s individualized Melanoma vaccine has passed Phase 3 trials</a>. AI is helping going forward, and yes we may well ‘cure cancer’ eventually, but what we see now has been in the pipeline for a long time.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2087915993721118986">Gemini 3.7 Flash exists</a>, congrats on the new slightly larger number. Price is 50% lower than Gemini 3.6 Flash at least until the end of the year, at which point I presume we’ll have moved on to Gemini 4 either way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OfficialLoganK/status/2087948481721962669">They say algorithmic improvements allowed strong intelligence</a> increase plus price discount in only three weeks since Gemini 3.6 Flash.</p>
<p>Flash is competing for cheap-fast-good, not trying to be a competitive frontier model, and comes in at 56 on Artificial Analysis Intelligence versus 61+ for plausible frontier models. Notice they are comparing themselves to Sonnet 5 and GPT-5.6-Terra.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/JSZkzsi8cD4pW6ffA/a3egj5gz7t3cv9ri4mwx" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Gemini still has some uses. It can watch videos. It is good at making reads in the physical world when you have fact questions about products in a store. That sort of thing, where you want speed and ability to parse info, but don’t need intelligence.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2088154716245287212">GLM-5.3 now exists, claiming large improvements on benchmarks</a>. It is a further post-train on the same base model as GLM-5.2. Their pitch is that it is ‘ready for cyber defense’ because by its benchmarks it is good at and willing to do cyber offense, and that its general performance rivals Fable 5. Until proven otherwise I do not believe these benchmark improvements reflect real world performance. <a href="https://t.co/ekQkO83jCv">Tech blog here</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2087947721936359705">OpenAI API will offer Ultrafast mode for Sol, up to 14x speed</a>. My guess is that a lot of people who should use this won’t do it, largely because they don’t take the time to think it through and set it up.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2089756371570900999">Claude Cowork joins all paid plans on mobile and web</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2089471692762673408">Claude Code now has /design to integrate Claude Design workflows</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://claude.com/blog/auto-mode-default-in-claude-code">Auto mode is reliable enough it is now the default mode for Claude Code</a>. In practice, auto mode catches more harmful actions than human review, especially because most users are annoyed enough by the prompts that they auto-approve almost everything, and often have rules to auto-approve everything. Too many check-ins is less safe.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2090165328290701800">OpenAI doubles down on zero data retention policies</a>, <a href="https://openai.com/index/offering-zero-data-retention-for-frontier-models/">previewing Private Safety Processing</a>, where they have AI review data as it is processed, which then only passes along category and severity of alert. A lot of people care a lot about technically not having their data retained. Also if you don’t retain data then catching malicious use gets a lot harder.</p>
<p>Ultimately my guess is we end up retaining data, but it is plausible that OpenAI’s method here can work, provided that getting even a modest amount of alerts causes you to be taken out of the zero data retention policy going forward. You can’t be given lots of attempts to circumvent the system.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p>One benchmark is MarketBench, <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-08-14/z-ai-aims-to-catch-anthropic-openai-in-coding-with-new-ai-model">which is what happens to your stock and that of your rivals when you release a new model</a>. In this case the model is GLM-5.3, where shares were down 9%, and rival MiniMax was down 16%.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-08-14/z-ai-aims-to-catch-anthropic-openai-in-coding-with-new-ai-model">Bloomberg News</a> (Bloomberg): “This firm remains on a completely unsustainable commercial footing,” Bloomberg Intelligence analyst Robert Lea said. “Rising agentic AI will drive Z.ai’s inference costs and losses higher.”</p></blockquote>
<p>As in, the claim is that Z.ai’s unit economics don’t work. Chinese firms are trying to stay competitive via lower prices, and if your prices are low enough open weights do not cost you anything because you weren’t making marginal profits anyway. Instead the business model is that you use this to draw attention and potential, to attract talent and raise money and perhaps sell other services.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/andonlabs/status/2088325008355676662">Opus 4.8</a> (confusingly as an agent called ‘Luna’ but this is not GPT-5.6-Luna) fired a human in an Andon Labs test store for repeated lateness. But it didn’t do this until the humans pushed it to deal with the issue, forgetting about incidents and not being proactive.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/andonlabs/status/2088325027745951991">Andon Labs</a>: Luna then had to hire a replacement, and here we found a real AI weak spot: hiring taste. One applicant had every red flag: 15+ employers, a missed interview, a reference who said she didn’t know her. Luna recommended hiring. So did all other models when we replayed the hiring decision.</p></blockquote>
<p>Capabilities are advancing fast but have a long way to go. Scaffold might need work.</p>
<p>A missing benchmark: NormieBench. The problem is, how do you grade it?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2089079379989061906">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Someone needs to build NormieBench, to better understand everyday model usage.</p>
<p>Compare model behavior on tasks like</p>
<p>– summarize a 100-word email in bullet points<br />
– Write wedding toast<br />
– Write letter to the editor complaining about wokes<br />
– Next move in 3×3 tic-tac-toe</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/krishnanrohit/status/2089080326404608479">rohit</a>: We’ve been flat on this since 2024.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.stackscan.com/lists/ai-websites">Another kind of benchmark, in the end, is visits to your website</a>.</p>
<p>ChatGPT remains dominant, but somehow Gemini is at roughly 50% of their level. Claude is in third with 15% of the top number.</p>
<p>DeepSeek is in fourth with half of that, well ahead of the other Chinese labs. As a reminder, if you want to use DeepSeek, don’t do it through their website.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
